# About the Author

**Seth Reichelson** is a 2015 White House Champion of Change, College Board Summer Institute Teacher, and IBM Champion. As a Computer Science teacher at Lake Brantley High School and Ocoee High School, he grew his computer program with grassroots activism. Through his unflappable likability and out-of-the-box implementation of ideas, he enlivened the class with quirky but accessible projects that appeal to all students. Putting his charismatic legitimacy to paper truly has been an epic adventure for all involved, including his Labradors. Seth Reichelson is a longtime high school teacher and Labrador retriever enthusiast.

# [How to Use This Book](#04_Contents.xhtml#rsec1)

This book is focused only on you getting correct answers on the AP exam. Topics are covered in the same depth and scope as the AP exam. This book offers a comprehensive review of the Enduring Understandings, Key Concepts, Learning Objectives, and Essential Knowledge taught in the AP Computer Science Principles course. Each element of this book is designed to prepare you for the exam.

## **Diagnostic Test**

First, take the Diagnostic Test to gain an understanding of your strengths and weaknesses. It is a seventy-question complete test with detailed answer explanations.

## **Create Performance Tasks**

This book includes six full sample Create Performance Tasks with complete written reports. All programs contain the requirements to fully score on the create task rubric. Programs are written in both graphical and text-based languages.

## **Review and Practice**

Chapters are arranged in order of the big ideas with two chapters dedicated to algorithms. Seven hundred and sixty-five multiple-choice questions written in AP format cover all topics on the exam. All multiple-choice questions in this text include detailed answer explanations.

## **Practice Tests**

The final section of the book offers the opportunity to take two full-length practice tests that include all question types found on the actual exam. A comprehensive answer explanation is provided for each multiple-choice question.

## **Online Practice**

In addition to the diagnostic test and two practice tests within this book, there are also three full-length online practice exams. You may take these exams in practice (untimed) mode or in timed mode. All questions include detailed answer explanations.

## **For Students**

This review book highlights all concepts in each chapter and explains them by giving content examples. How you use the book depends on how your school offers the course. Nevertheless, by answering the review questions at the end of each chapter and by taking the practice tests, you will have an indication of how well you will do on the actual exam.

## **For Teachers**

This book can be used as a stand-alone textbook or in combination with any of the College Board approved curriculums.

# [BARRON’S ESSENTIAL 5](#04_Contents.xhtml#rsec2)

As you review the content in this book and work toward earning that “5” on your AP Computer Science Principles Exam, here are five essentials that you **MUST** know above everything else:

 
| **1** || Implement all programming requirements for the Create Performance Task. Your program does not need to be a masterpiece, but it does need to contain the requirements. Make sure your submitted program contains a procedure, parameters, list, iteration, selection, and sequence. Use examples provided in this book to familiarize yourself with how the coding requirements can be implemented in your program. A 5000-line program that does not contain all the requirements will score lower than a 19-line program that does. |
| --- | --- |
| **2** || Understand what you programmed and be able to answer College Board questions in the written report. While College Board questions are vague, what they expect for answers is extremely specific. No partial credit is given. Use the examples and nonexamples provided in this book to understand how the College Board wants you to answer the written questions. |
| **3** || Be aware of the rules of syntax for the pseudocode used on the multiple-choice exam. The programming language used on the exam is unique to the College Board and can conflict with the rules for the programming language you used in class. For example, the index of a list begins with 1 on the exam but in many languages the index begins with 0. |
| **4** || Become familiar with the type of questions asked on the exam. For example, your multiple-choice exam will contain robot questions, tracing problems, finding error problems, converting number questions, looping questions, and others. Use the hundreds of example problems included in this book to prepare. |
| **5** || Understand the scope of questions used on the exam. Many topics on this exam are complex but what is tested on the exam is not complex. For example, the internet is a complex topic and could take months to cover to fully understand the topic. However, the scope and depth of internet questions included on this test are limited. This book only covers what is on the test. |

# [**Preface**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rsec3)

This book is aimed at students taking the AP Computer Science Principles exam and is designed to help students prepare for exam topics, regardless of what computer language or method they were taught.

The book provides in-depth instructions on how to complete the Create Performance Task with six complete written and coded examples. We have included sample responses that earn high scores and sample responses that earn low scores. The provided sample responses are intended to serve as guiding resources only. Do NOT copy the sample responses! The College Board does rigorous checks for plagiarism.

The questions and examples all reflect the style of recent exam questions and cover the essential knowledge topics outlined by the College Board. For the benefit of students and instructors, we have included one diagnostic test and two practice exams in this book, with three more practice exams available online.

#  

# [**Introduction**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rsec4)

## [**General Information About the Exam**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev1)

The AP Computer Science Principles exam is a two-hour multiple-choice exam with 70 questions covering five Big Ideas accompanied by a performance task containing three submissions. The College Board recommends you submit your performance task on April 15, with a due date of approximately April 30. After your teacher sets up your College Board account, you will be able to submit your performance task starting December 3. I recommend you submit your performance task well before the April 30 deadline.

Because the College Board is constantly tweaking the dates, check for current dates at *[https://apstudent.collegeboard.org/apcourse/ap-computer-science-principles](https://apstudent.collegeboard.org/apcourse/ap-computer-science-principles)*

### **Content of Class**

Performance task: 30% of AP Grade

Guaranteed 12 hours of classroom time to finish

Program Code

Written Responses

Video of at least one significant part of your program running

End of Course exam: 70% of AP Grade

2 hours

70 multiple-choice questions, paper and pencil exam

Creative Development 10–13%

Data 17–22%

Algorithms and Programming 30–35%

Computer Systems and Networks 11–15%

Impact of Computing 21–26%

Some exam questions may be aligned to more than one learning objective. For example, a question on programming might implement an algorithm and contain an abstraction.

## [**Hints for Taking the Exam**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev2)

### **The Multiple-Choice Section**

The multiple-choice questions are worth 70% of your score and are administrated at the end of the school year. There is no penalty for wrong answers. So answer every question, even if you have to guess.

You have a little more than 1.7 minutes per question. You should have enough time to answer all questions on this test, and you will most likely have enough time at the end to go back to difficult questions.

The 70 questions are fatiguing. You should practice at least one of the sample tests given in one sitting. Robot and programming questions can be simplified by hand-tracing the code.

People score the performance task, while the EOC exam is scored by machine. The test is independent of the computer language used, but there are both text-based programming questions and block-based programming questions.

### **The Performance Task**

Details on how to create a College Board account and how to submit your performance task can be found at *[https://apstudent.collegeboard.org/takingtheexam/about-digital-portfolio](https://apstudent.collegeboard.org/takingtheexam/about-digital-portfolio)*

### **Create Task**

Allow your own interests to drive your choice of computing program.

Before you start writing, make sure you have read and understand the questions and how these questions are graded. There are small changes in the question each year, and the newest versions of both the questions and the scoring criteria can be found at *[https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/pdf/ap-csp-student-task-directions.pdf](https://apcentral.collegeboard.org/pdf/ap-csp-student-task-directions.pdf)*

Your program code should represent the results of explorations that go beyond the examples presented in class.

Again, the deadline for submission is April 30. The College Board recommends submitting by April 15.

# [**Diagnostic Test**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev2a)

The exam that follows has the same format as that used on the actual AP exam. There are two ways you may use it:

1.Use it as a diagnostic test before you start reviewing. At the end of the test is a diagnostic chart that relates each question to sections that you should review. In addition, complete explanations are provided for each solution.

2.You can also use it as a practice exam when you have completed your review.

# [**Diagnostic Test**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev3a)

TIME: 120 MINUTES

70 QUESTIONS

**DIRECTIONS:** Each of the questions or incomplete statements below is followed by four suggested answers or completions. Select the one that is best in each case and fill in the appropriate letter in the corresponding space on the answer sheet.

[1](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia1).Consider the following code segment, which uses the variables *a* and *c*.

*a* ← 3

*a* ← *a* + 5

*c* ← 3

*a* ← *c* + *a*

DISPLAY(a)

DISPLAY(c)

What is displayed when running the code segment?

(A)3 3

(B)3 8

(C)11 3

(D)3 11

[2](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia2).When writing a program, what is true about program documentation?

I.Program documentation is useful while writing the program.

II.Program documentation is useful after the program is written.

III.Program documentation is not useful when run speed is a factor.

(A)I only

(B)I and II only

(C)II only

(D)I, II, and III

[3](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia3).An image stored on a computer contains pixels that represent how bright the red, green, and blue values are. The most common format for pixels is to represent the red, green, and blue, using 8 bits, which vary from 0 to 255. If the current red value is 10011101, what would be the new value in binary if the red value is increased by 4 in decimal?

(A)157[BIN]

(B)0100[BIN]

(C)10011111[BIN]

(D)10100001[BIN]

[4](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia4).A programmer is writing software for a diverse set of users. While the program runs, the programmer wants to meet the needs of all her customers and reduce potential bias in her algorithms. What would be the benefit of collaborating with colleagues?

(A)When collaborating, too many people try to lead the group and not enough members are willing to take a backseat, which will delay the release of the program.

(B)Conflicts in the working styles within the group will decrease staff morale.

(C)With multiple people looking over code, errors can be quickly fixed.

(D)Multiple people with different life experiences can see the code and give perspective on the ways the original programmer can decrease bias.

[5](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia5).Which of the following examples **LEAST** likely indicates a phishing attack?

(A)An email indicates that a password is expiring and asks you to click a link to renew your password.

(B)An email from a familiar company, which has the exact look of previous emails from this company, reports that the current credit card information on file has expired and has a link for you to reenter credit card information.

(C)An email from the IRS contains the correct IRS logo and asks you to submit your social security number so the IRS can mail an additional tax refund. Additionally, the email contains a warning that if this information is not filled out within 30 days, the refund will be lost.

(D)An email from your credit card company with the correct bank logo indicates that there has been unusual activity on your credit card and to call the number on your card to confirm the purchase.

[6](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia6).With the lowering of the digital divide and more products using the internet, the world is running out of IP addresses. The current plan is to switch from IPv4, which holds 32 bits, to IPv6, which holds 128 bits.

With the increase from 32 bits to 128 bits, what is the resulting increase in possible IP addresses?

(A)2 × 96

(B)2^(96)

(C)96²

(D)96

(B)Syntax error on line 4

(C)Runtime error on line 4

I.54

II.63

III.64

IV.89

(A)All of the above

(B)I, II, and IV only

(C)I and III only

(D)I and II only

**Questions 10–14 refer to the information below.**

A restaurant uses phones to take reservations and to place to-go orders. The restaurant is planning to update its system to provide more options to customers while decreasing the ordering time. Customers will now interact with a website instead of an actual person.

The upgraded system (but not the original system) stores all information from the web interactions for future references. This includes food items ordered, address, telephone number, and credit card info.

The original system and the upgraded system are described in the following flowcharts. Each flowchart uses the following blocks.

 
| **Block** || **Explanation** |
| --- | --- |
| Oval || The start of the algorithm |
| --- | --- |
| Parallelogram || An input or output step |
| Diamond || A conditional or decision step, where execution proceeds to the side labeled “Yes” if the answer to the question is yes and to the side labeled “No” if the answer to the question is no |
| Rectangle || The result of the algorithm |

Original System

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0010-01.jpg)

Upgraded System

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0011-01.jpg)

[10](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia10).Which of the following input data is needed by the upgraded system but was **NOT** needed by the original system?

(A)Name

(B)Order

(C)Time of pickup or reservation

(D)Customer’s car make and model

[11](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia11).The upgraded system uses data not supplied by the customer. The new site is supposed to increase sales by suggesting products based on the order (would you like fries with that?) and decreasing processing time. Which of the following data is **LEAST** likely to be included in the web-based ordering system?

(A)IP address of the customer

(B)Previous list of food orders

(C)A list of popular items frequently added to orders

(D)Time to prepare order

[12](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia12).Which of the following is **MOST** likely a data privacy concern of the upgraded system?

(A)Storing credit card information can provide the user with a better experience by auto filling in data to speed up ordering.

(B)Customers are more likely to make reservations.

(C)Storing customer information will increase the likelihood of a phishing attack by rogue hackers.

(D)Customers’ personal data can be compromised if hackers gain access into the ordering system.

[13](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia13).Of the following potential benefits, which is **LEAST** likely to be provided by the upgraded system?

(A)Customers being more likely to order to-go orders

(B)An increase in food per order

(C)An increase in awareness of the restaurant

(D)A decrease in incorrect orders and delivery addresses

[14](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia14).Which of the following may be an unintended effect of the upgraded system?

(A)Reservations for the restaurant will increase.

(B)Customers giving the suggested tips will increase income for the staff.

(C)Reservations will be primarily made by customers who can afford internet access.

(D)Repeat business will increase.

[15](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia15).In 2013, hackers tricked a third-party vendor into clicking a malicious link that installed software that compromised the credit card numbers and other personal information for millions of customers of Target, a large retail chain. What category of cyber attack does the Target hack fit?

(A)Phishing attack

(B)Keylogging

(C)Rogue access point

(D)Packet metadata

| T || F || T |
| F || T || F |
| F || F || F |

[18](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia18).Consider the following procedure called mystery, which is intended to display the number of times a number target appears in a list.

Which of the following best describes the behavior of the procedure?

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0013-02.jpg)

**Select two answers.**

(B)The program never correctly displays the correct value for count.

[19](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia19).Two lists, list1 and list2, contain unique eight-number student IDs for Lake Brantley High School. An administrator for the school wants to create one list so the one list can be searched using a binary search.

The following abstractions are available to create the new searchable list.

 
| **Abstraction** || **Explanation** |
| --- | --- |
| Sort(list) || Sorts a list from lowest to highest number |
| Combine(list1, list2) || Creates a new list by combining the elements of list1 followed by the elements of list2. |
| BinarySearch(list, value) || Returns the index of value in a list using a binary search. This abstraction will return a −1 if the value is not in the list. |

Which of the following code segments will correctly search for a value using a binary search?

(A)`list1` ← `Sort(list1)`

`list2` ← `Sort(list2)`

`resultList` ← `combine(list1, list2)`

`BinarySearch(resultList, value)`

(B)`resultList` ← `combine(list1, list2)`

`BinarySearch(resultList, value)`

(C)`resultList` ← `combine(list1, list2)`

`resultList` ← `Sort(resultList)`

`BinarySearch(resultList, value)`

(D)`BinarySearch(list1, value)`

`BinarySearch(list2, value)`

[1](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-1).**(C)**When answering program questions, a trace table is helpful in keeping track of variables.

 
| `a` || `c` |
| --- | --- |
| 3 || 3 |
| 8 ||   |
| 11 ||   |

(A)Although the initial value of *a* is 3, it updates to 8 and then 11.

(B)Variable *c* is initialized at 3 and is not updated in this problem.

(C)Correct. Variables *a* and *c* follow the above trace table, and the DISPLAY method displays the value contained in *a* followed by a space and then the value contained in *c* followed by a space.

(D)Although the values of *a* and *c* follow the trace table, the order of the DISPLAY method is *a* first and then *c*.

[2](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-2).**(B)**Program documentation is always useful in all stages of programming before, during, and after. Documentation before programming helps with planning the algorithms. Documentation during the development of the program helps with organization and collaboration. Documentation after programming helps the coder update the program after completion.

(A)Program documentation is useful while writing the program and is also useful before and after the program is written.

(B)Correct.

(C)Program documentation is useful after writing the program and is also useful before and during the program development.

(D)Program documentation does not affect the run speed of programs.

[3](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-3).**(D)**Step 1. Convert 10011101[BIN] to [DEC].

![Images](OEBPS/images/t0035-01.jpg)

128 + 16 + 8 + 4 + 1 = 157

Step 2. Add the decimal numbers.

157 + 4 = 161

Step 3. Convert 161[DEC] to binary.

![Images](OEBPS/images/t0035-02.jpg)

161 − 128 = 33

33 − 21 = 1

1 − 1 = 0

Answer: 10100001[BIN]

[4](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-4).**(D)**Programs can be biased based on who builds them, how they are developed, and how they are ultimately used. [5](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-5).**(D)**Phishing, viruses, and other attacks have both human and software components and can have potentially devastating effects.

(A)This is likely a phishing attack. Hackers can use the entered password to gain access to personal accounts.

(B)The format of a company’s email can be imitated. Hackers can use credit card information to purchase things and not pay for them.

(C)The format for an IRS email can be imitated. The promise of an additional tax refund is used to motivate victims to reply with the correct information.

(D)Correct. The credit card company is asking you to call a number already printed on your card.

[6](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-6).**(B)**The number of devices that could use an IP address has grown so fast that a new protocol (IPv6) has been established, increasing the bits used to store an IP address from 32 bits to 128 bits.

Bits can hold 2^((number of bits)) values. For example, 3 bits can hold 2³ values, 2³ = 2 × 2 × 2 = 8 values.

Increasing 32 bits to 128 bits would increase the size of the possible values by 2^(128) − 2^(32) = 2^(96).

Calculating the value of 2^(96) is beyond the scope of this class, but for the curious it is equal to 7.922816251426434e[28].

(A)2 × 96 = 192, which is considerably smaller than 2^(96).

(B)Correct.

(C)96² = 9,216, which is considerably smaller than 2^(96).

(D)96 is considerably smaller than 2^(96).

[7](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-7).**(C)**Logic error is a mistake in the algorithm or program that causes it to behave incorrectly or unexpectedly. Choices (A) and (B) are incorrect because line 1 does not contain any errors. A syntax error is a mistake in the program where the rules of the programming language are not followed. Although line 4 does contain an error, the rules of the language are being followed. A runtime error is a mistake in the program that occurs during the execution of a program. Programming languages define their own runtime errors.

The language used on the AP exam starts indexes at 1, not 0. Since index 0 is unreachable, a runtime error has occurred.

[8](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-8).**(C)**The variable *a* is initialized as Friend before it enters the For Each loop. Once in the loop, Cat, Meow, Cow, and Bark are concatenated to the end of *a*. After the loop, the variable *a* contains Friend Cat Meow Cow Bark. After the loop, the word Friend is concatenated the end of *a*. The new value of *a* is now Friend Cat Meow Cow Bark Friend. This new value is returned and displayed.

 
| **a** || **Concatenation** || **Return** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Friend ||   ||   |
| Friend || Cat ||   |
| Friend Cat || Meow ||   |
| Friend Cat Meow || Cow ||   |
| Friend Cat Meow Cow || Bark ||   |
| Friend Cat Meow Cow Bark || Friend ||   |
| Friend Cat Meow Cow Bark Friend ||   || Friend Cat Meow Cow Bark Friend |

[9](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-9).**(D)**Six bits can store 2⁶ numbers: 2⁶ = 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 64. This means that 6 bits can store 64 numbers, from 0 to 63.

An overflow error is an error that occurs when trying to store too large of a number. The largest number stored using 6 bits is 63, option (II). Since option (I), 54, is less than the cutoff number, option (I) will **NOT** cause an overflow error.

(A)64 and 89 = overflow error

(B)89 = overflow error

(C)64 = overflow error

(D)Correct

[22](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-22).**(B)**A binary search needs to be performed with a **sorted list**. A binary search starts from the middle and reduces the number of elements to check by 1/2 every pass.

In the first list, the binary search would start at the 13th letter:

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0039-02.jpg)

The letter *w* is being searched; therefore, the first half of the list is left off. The new list has 13 objects.

Dividing 13 in half yields 6.5 (13/2 = 6.5), which is rounded to 7.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0040-01.jpg)

Again, because the letter *w* is being searched, the first half of the list is eliminated. The new list has six objects.

Dividing 6 in half yields 3 (6/2 = 3).

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0040-02.jpg)

A binary search in a sorted list, therefore, requires three passes to find the letter *w*.

(A)Two passes of the binary search would result in the letter *t*.

(B)Correct.

(C)If a linear search was being done, 23 would be correct. A linear search starts from the left end of the data structure and checks every letter in order from left to right.

(D)Every pass in a binary search reduces the number of searchable items by half. The worst-case scenario would be 5 in this data structure.

[23](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-23).**(D)**In a linear search, the data structure **does not have to be sorted**. A linear search starts at the first letter and checks each consecutive letter from left to right.

\[a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, j, k, l, m, n, o, p, q, r, s, t, u, v, w, x, y, z\]

Using this data structure, it would take 24 passes to find the correct letter.

(A)The third pass using a linear search would result in the letter *c*.

(B)The fourth pass using a linear search would result in the letter *d*.

(C)The 23rd pass using a linear search would result in the letter *w*.

(D)Correct.

(B)It probably requires more testing but saves time in the long run.

(C)Nothing *always* produces desired output on Earth.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f0048-01.jpg)

(B)num > 13 does not include the number 13.

(C)num < 13 is not included in the num > 13 or num = 13 subset.

(D)A number cannot be both greater than 13 and equal to 13 at the same time.

(C)Packets are reassembled after traveling to the designated computer.

(D)Correct. First your data are chopped up into small packets. The packets then enter the internet through your internet service provider (ISP). Those packets are then routed throughout the internet. Finally, the packets are reassembled into a coherent message.

[63](#09_Diagnostic.xhtml#dia-63).**(C)**Abstractions should reduce the level of complexity of a program.

A programmer can override existing code to extend or change abstractions.

Although abstractions should be designed to be used in multiple programs, they do not guarantee fewer lines of code are used. The user of the program will care about run time and memory space; the lines of code should not be a factor.

Abstractions are built to be in the background and hidden. Although the source code can potentially be seen, abstractions are designed to be in the background.

Heuristic solutions are designed to save time or memory. Taking bits out of files would not make the solution heuristic.

(D)is incorrect because nothing is guaranteed to work.

## [**General Requirements**](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev5)

The Create Performance Task is worth 30 percent of your AP exam. If you pay careful attention to the prompts and rubric, no points should be lost on this section. The prompts and rubric can change slightly, and the current version can be found on *[apcentral.collegeboard.org](http://apcentral.collegeboard.org)*. Make sure you are using the most current version of the rubric.

Programming is a collaborative and creative process that brings ideas to life through the development of software. Programs can help solve problems, enable innovations, or express personal interests. In this performance task, you will be developing a program of your choice. Your development process should include iteratively designing, implementing, and testing your program. You are strongly encouraged to work with another student in your class for parts of this program.

WARNING: You **can** collaborate with your partner(s) on the development of the program only. The written response and the video that you submit for this performance task must be completed **individually**, without any collaboration with your partner(s) or anyone else.

Your teacher must guarantee at least **9 hours** of class time and unlimited out of class time to complete and submit the following:

■Final program code (created independently or collaboratively)

■A video that displays the running of your program and demonstrates functionality you developed (created independently)

■Student-authored Personalized Project Reference

Your video must show:

■Input to your program

■At least one aspect of the functionality of your program

■Output produced by your program

Your video must NOT show:

■Any distinguishing information about yourself

■Voice narration (although text captions are encouraged)

Your video must meet the following specifications:

■be stored in .mp4, .wmv, .avi, or .mov format

■be no more than 1 minute in length

■be no more than 30 MB in file size

## [Program Requirements](#04_Contents.xhtml#rlev6)

Your program must demonstrate a variety of capabilities and implement several different language features that, when combined, produce a result that cannot be easily accomplished without computing tools and techniques. Your program should draw upon a combination of mathematical and logical concepts, such as use of numbers, variables, mathematical expressions with arithmetic operators, logical and Boolean operators and expressions, decision statements, iteration, and collections.

Your program must demonstrate:

■Instructions for **input** from one of the following:

∘The user (including user actions that trigger events)

∘A device

∘An online data streams

∘A file

■Use of at least one **list** (or other collection type) to represent a collection of data that is stored and used to manage program complexity and help fulfill the programs purpose. A list can be an Array, ArrayList, LinkedList, etc.

■At least one **procedure** (abstraction, method, subroutine) that contributes to the program’s intended purpose, where you have defined:

∘The procedure’s name

∘The return type (if necessary)

∘**One or more parameters**

∘An algorithm that includes **sequencing, selection, and iteration**, which is in the body of the selected procedure

∘Calls to your student-developed procedure

∘Instructions for output (tactile, audible, visual, or textual) based on input and program functionality

There is NO designated programming language for AP Computer Science Principles. Students may choose a programming language learned while taking the course to complete the task, or they may select a different programming language that they are familiar with outside of class.

Sample languages that can be used for the Create Performance Task:

![Images](OEBPS/images/f002-01.jpg)

WARNING: HTML is NOT an accepted language for the Create Performance Task.

As long as the language is able to create a list and call a procedure that takes in a parameter, you can use it. There is no scoring difference between using a low-level language or a high-level language.

### Preprogramming Checklist

When selecting the program focus, students should ensure that their program will be sophisticated enough to integrate mathematical and logical concepts, develop abstraction, use a list, and implement algorithms.

A 500-line program will not necessarily score higher than a 30-line program. In fact, a 500-line program that does not iterate or have a procedure (can be called abstractions, functions, or methods) that takes in a parameter will score lower than the 30-line program if the 30-line program contains all the correct elements.

The programming topic is completely up to you and should be something that interests you. However, you need to account for the limited number of class time hours. If you want to write the new version of *Halo,* go for it. However, *Halo* will not be able to be written in the provided 12 hours. Think of the scope of your program before diving in.

While writing the program, you should be aware that your end program requires instructions for input, a list, a procedure that takes in a parameter, and an algorithm that includes sequencing, selection, and iteration.

**Programming Checklist to Hit the Rubric**

 
| Does your program have at least one list (a list can also be an array, database, or set) that is integral to the function of your program? || yes/no |
| --- | --- |
| Are the data stored in the list vital to the function of the program? || yes/no |
| Is the program showing how the data are loaded into the list? || yes/no |
| Are the data in the list accessed by iteration (not directly accessed by index)? || yes/no |
| Does your program take in and use input from the user, device, or online data stream or a file? || yes/no |
| Does your program contain a procedure that takes in parameters? Are those parameters being used? || yes/no |

### **Written Questions 1– 4**

You will have access to your Personalized Project Reference, and write responses to four prompts related to your program code and the code contained in this reference sheet. One question from each of the categories listed below will appear on the end-of-course exam. The specific prompts will vary across the different versions of the exam.

**Question 1:**

Program Design, Function, and Purpose

Students should be prepared to respond to prompts about their program that assess any of the following learning objectives:

■Describe the purpose of computing innovation.

■Explain how a program or code segment functions.

■Identify input(s) to a program.

■Identify output(s) produced by a program.

■Develop a program using a development process.

■Design a program and its user interface.

■Describe the purpose of a code segment or program by writing documentation.

**Question 2:**

Algorithm Development

Students should be prepared to respond to prompts about their program that assess any of the following learning objectives:

■Explain how a program or code segment functions.

■Evaluate expressions that use relational operators.

■Evaluate expressions that use logic operators.

■Determine the result of conditional statements.

■Express an algorithm that uses iteration without using a programming language.

■Determine the result or side effect of iteration statements.

■Compare multiple algorithms to determine if they yield the same side effect or result.

■Create algorithms.

■Combine and modify existing algorithms.

**Question 3:**

Errors and Testing

Students should be prepared to respond to prompts about their program that assess any of the following learning objectives:

■Identify the error.

■Correct the error.

■Identify inputs and corresponding expected outputs or behaviors that can be used to check the correctness of an algorithm or program.

**Type of Errors That Could Be Tested**

■A **logic error** is a mistake in the algorithm or program that causes it to behave incorrectly or unexpectedly.

Examples:

Misplacing the order of operations in a mathematical expression.

Creating loops with incorrect entry or exit conditions.

■A **syntax error** is a mistake in the program where the rules of the programming language are not followed.

Examples:

Forgetting a semicolon in Java or a colon in Python.

Using incorrect capitalization for variable names.

■A **run-time error** is a mistake in the program that occurs during the execution of a program. Programming languages define their own runtime errors.

Examples:

Attempting to divide by zero.

Accessing a list element with an index that exceeds the list bounds.

■An **overflow error** is an error that occurs when a computer attempts to handle a number that is outside of the defined range of values.

Examples:

If a language uses 4 bits to store an integer the maximum value would be 2^n - 1 = 15

If a language uses 6 bits to store an integer the maximum value would be 2^n - 1 = 63

**Question 4:**

Data and Procedural Abstraction

Students should be prepared to respond to prompts about their program that assess any of the following learning objectives:

■Develop data abstraction using lists to store multiple elements.

■Explain how the use of data abstraction manages complexity in program code.

■Write iteration statements to traverse a list.

■Determine the result of an algorithm that includes list traversals.

■Explain how the use of procedural abstraction manages complexity in a program.

### **Answer questions 1–17 based on the following procedure.**

**Note:** Like the AP exam, the list used in the below code segment will start at index 1.

The function of the following code segment is to display “Victory” if the user enters a color that is contained in a rainbow.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f054-01.jpg)

**Program Design, Function, and Purpose**

1.Identify an expected user of your program. Describe one way your program’s design meets the needs of this user.

My program is designed for preteens interested in learning about rainbow colors. My program achieves this by quizzing the user on diverse colors and indicating whether each color is part of the rainbow.

2.Explain how a program or code segment functions.

The function of my program is to prompt the user to input a color. The program will then go through a list containing all the colors of the rainbow. If the input color is found in the list, the program displays “Victory.” If the input color does not match any in the rainbow list, it will display “Fail.”

3.Identify the input/output of the program.

The user provides an input, which is a color. If the input is found in the words list, the output is “Victory.” However, if the input is not present in the words list, the output is “Fail.”

**Algorithm Development**

4.Identify the number of times the body of your iteration statement will execute.

The loop in my program will run for 7 iterations.

5.Describe a condition or error that would cause your iteration statement to not terminate and cause an infinite loop. If no such condition or error exists, explain how the loop could be modified to cause an infinite loop.

The loop employed in my program is set to be executed 7 times, independent of the program’s outcome. This design choice ensures that an infinite loop cannot occur.

However, if I change the loop from a “REPEAT 7 TIMES” to a REPEAT UNTIL user = words\[count\]” where user is not contained in the words list an infinite loop would occur.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f055-01.jpg)

6.Describe two calls to the procedure in the supplied program. Each call must pass a different argument(s) that causes a different segment of code in the algorithm to execute, and describe what conditions are being tested and the result of each call.

Assume that words contain (“red”,“orange”,“yellow”,“green”,“blue”,“indigo”,“violet”) and user contains “blue.” The first call would be

![Images](OEBPS/images/f055-02.jpg)

Assume that words contain (“red”,“orange”,“yellow”,“green”,“blue”,“indigo”,“violet”) and user contains “purple.” The second call would be

![Images](OEBPS/images/f055-03.jpg)

The first call would test to see if “blue” is contained in the words list.

The second call would test to see if “purple” is contained in the words list.

The result of the first call is “Victory” will be displayed.

The result of the second call is “Fail” will be displayed.

7.Describe what the data contained in the list represents in your program.

My list holds 7 strings, each representing a color found in the rainbow.

**Errors and Testing**

8.Outline a modification that would result in a runtime error.

Attempting to access an array element with an index that falls outside the acceptable range of indices can lead to an error. For instance, within the mystery procedure, the words list contains seven elements indexed from 1 to 7. Trying to access an element at index 8 or index 0 would result in a runtime error. If the “REPEAT 7 times” command were changed to “REPEAT 8 times,” it would result in “words\[count\]” to contain words\[8\], which exceeds the valid bounds of the words list.

9.Outline a modification that would result in a logical error.

Exchanging the line “RETURN\[“Victory”\]” with the line “RETURN\[“Fail”\]” would result in incorrect behavior for the algorithm. This swap would cause the algorithm to return “Fail” when the word contained in the user input is in the words list, and “Victory” when the word in the user parameter is not in the list. This outcome is contrary to the expected behavior.

10.Outline a modification that would result in a syntax error.

Setting the initial value of count as “count = 1” rather than “count <− 1” in the code would result in a syntax error.

11.Outline a modification that would result in an overflow error.

If the data type of count were an 8-bit integer, the maximum value it could hold without triggering an overflow error would be 2^n - 1, which, in this specific case, is 256 - 1, equal to 255. Consequently, attempting to store the number 278 in `count` would result in an overflow error.

12.Describe the procedure used to test for correctness of your algorithm.

When testing with the scenario where `words = [“yellow”, “orange”, “blue”, “red”]` and `user = “blue”`, the expected outcome is “Victory,” and indeed, it was. In another test case with `words = [“yellow”, “orange”, “blue”, “red”]` and `user = “pink”`, the anticipated result is “Fail,” and the program delivered as expected.

**Data and Procedural Abstraction**

13.Elaborate on the role of the list within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a list, and discuss how employing the list has simplified the program’s complexity.

In my program, I employed a list named “planets,” which comprises nine strings signifying the names of the celestial bodies in our solar system. These planet names are stored in the list with indices ranging from 0 to 8. Utilizing this list allowed me to minimize the number of variables I needed to create as I only had to name one variable, “planets,” instead of having to define nine individual variables for each planet. This reduction in code complexity, achieved by using a single variable to represent the list, significantly lowered the likelihood of errors in my program.

Moreover, through the utilization of my “planets” list, I could efficiently examine and verify if the user’s input matched any element within the list by employing a for loop in conjunction with just one if statement. Without employing a list, testing for equality among all nine planets would necessitate the creation of nine distinct if statements. This simplification of code complexity, facilitated by the use of a singular variable representing the list, considerably diminished the probability of errors in my program.

14.Elaborate on the role of procedural abstraction within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a procedural abstraction, and discuss how employing the procedural abstraction has simplified the program’s complexity.

By encapsulating the algorithm that checks if a word is present in a list within a procedure, I have significantly reduced the complexity of my program. This reduction is achieved by enabling me to reuse the algorithm at multiple points in my program without the need to duplicate the code. For instance, if I wish to verify the presence of five words in my list, I can simply call the procedure five times. Without this procedural approach, I would be compelled to write 20 lines of code instead of just 5.

Additionally, by utilizing procedures, errors related to word presence checks are localized within the procedure itself. In the absence of procedures, I would be required to scrutinize the entire program to pinpoint the source of any errors related to this functionality.

15.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called isEqual(value1, value2). The procedure returns true if the two parameters, value1 and value2, are equal in value and returns false otherwise. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses isEqual to count the number of times a certain value appears in your list. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else t write the program code.

1.Set the initial value of “total” to 0.

2.Set the initial value of count to 1.

3.Begin iterating through the “words” list.

4.For each item in the list, check if “user” is equal to “words\[count\],” where “count” varies from 1 to 7, and if a match is found, increase “total” by one.

5.Once all items in the list have been iterated through, return the cumulative “total” value.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f058-01.jpg)

16.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called compareTo(value1, value2). The procedure returns a negative number if value1 has a lower alphabetical order than value2, a positive value if value1 has a higher alphabetical order than value 2, and 0 if value1 has the same alphabetical order as value2. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses compareTo to count the number of times the word stored in the value user appears before each item in your list. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else to write the program code.

1.Initialize the value of total to 0.

2.Start the loop where it will iterate through the entire words list.

3.If compareTo user, an item from the words list returns a positive value, it means that user has a higher alphabetical order than the current list item. In this case, increase the total variable by one.

4.Repeat step 3 for the entire words list which in this case would be seven times.

5.End the loop.

6.Return the accumulated value of total.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f059-01.jpg)

17.Suppose you have access to a procedure named conCat(word1, word2), which concatenates two words. For example, if you call conCat(“Tiger”, “Cat”), it returns “TigerCat”. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses conCat to return a single word that contains all the words in your list combined into one large word. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else to write the program code.

1.Initialize the value of combinedWord to an empty string.

2.Start the loop where it will iterate through the entire words list.

3.conCat combinedWord with the current item in the list.

4.Repeat step 3 for the entire words list, which in this case would be seven times.

5.End the loop.

6.Return the accumulated value of combinedWord.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f060-01.jpg)

### Answer questions 18–32 based on the following procedure.

**Note:** Like the AP exam, the list used in the below code segment will start at index 1.

The function of the following code segment is to create a list of 10 random numbers from 1 to 10, and then return the number of correctly answered multiplication problems using the 10 random numbers.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f061-01.jpg)

**Program Design, Function, and Purpose**

18.Identify an expected user of your program. Describe one way your program’s design meets the needs of this user.

My program is designed to teach early math students how to multiply by quizzing the user on five random multiplication problems using numbers from 1 to 10.

19.Explain how a program or code segment functions.

The function of my program is to create a list with 10 random numbers from 1 to 10. The program will then display a multiplication problem and ask the user for the solution. The program then returns how many of the five problems were answered correctly.

20.Identify the input/output of the program.

Input is the five answers to multiplication problems. The output is the number of correct answers.

**Algorithm Development**

21.Identify the number of times the body of your iteration statement will execute.

The loop in my program will run for 5 iterations.

22.Describe a condition or error that would cause your iteration statement to not terminate and cause an infinite loop. If no such condition or error exists, explain how the loop could be modified to cause an infinite loop.

The loop employed in my program is set to be executed 5 times, independent of the program’s outcome. This design choice ensures that an infinite loop cannot occur.

However, if I were to alter the loop to a “REPEAT UNTIL” (a while loop) that continues looping until count is a negative number, and that value is always greater to or equal to zero, the procedure will be stuck in an infinite loop.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f062-01.jpg)

23.Describe what the data contained in the list represents in your program.

My list holds 10 random numbers from 1 to 10 which will be used in the five multiplication problems.

**Errors and Testing**

24.Outline a modification that would result in a runtime error.

Attempting to access an array element with an index that falls outside the acceptable range of indices can lead to an error. For instance, within the mathTest procedure, the nums list contains 10 numbers indexed from 1 to 10. Trying to access an element at index 11 or index 0 would result in a runtime error. If the “REPEAT 5 times” command were changed to “REPEAT 6 times,” it would result in trying to access nums\[11\] and nums\[12\], which exceeds the valid bounds of the nums list.

25.Outline a modification that would result in a logical error.

Changing the line of code that multiples the two values to addition would cause a logic error in the mathTest procedure.

26.Outline a modification that would result in a syntax error.

Setting the initial value of `count` as “count = 1” rather than “count <− 1” in the code would result in a syntax error.

27.Outline a modification that would result in an overflow error.

If the data type of `count` were an 8-bit integer, the maximum value it could hold without triggering an overflow error would be 2^n − 1, which, in this specific case, is 256 − 1, equal to 255. Consequently, attempting to store the number 278 in `count` would result in an overflow error.

28.Describe the procedure used to test for correctness of your algorithm.

If the list nums contains the values \[2,1,1,3,5,6,7,8,9,10\] and the input contains \[2,3,30,50,80\] the returned value should be 3.

**Data and Procedural Abstraction**

29.Elaborate on the role of the list within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a list, and discuss how employing the list has simplified the program’s complexity.

In my program, I employed a list named “nums,” which comprises 10 random integers from 1 to 10. These numbers are stored in the list with indices ranging from one to ten. Utilizing this list allowed me to minimize the number of variables I needed to create, as I only had to name one variable, “nums,” instead of having to define 10 individual variables for each planet. This reduction in code complexity, achieved by using a single variable to represent the list, significantly lowered the likelihood of errors in my program.

Moreover, through the utilization of my “nums” list, I could efficiently examine and verify if the user’s input matched any element within the list by employing a for loop in conjunction with just one if statement. Without employing a list, testing for equality among all 10 numbers would necessitate the creation of five distinct if statements. This simplification of code complexity, facilitated by the use of a singular variable representing the list, considerably diminished the probability of errors in my program.

30.Elaborate on the role of procedural abstraction within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a procedural abstraction, and discuss how employing the procedural abstraction has simplified the program’s complexity.

By encapsulating the algorithm that checks five multiplication problems in one procedure, I have significantly reduced the complexity of my program. This reduction is achieved by enabling me to reuse the algorithm at multiple points in my program without the need to duplicate the code. For instance, if I wish to use the test five additional times, I can simply call the procedure five times. Without this procedural approach, I would be compelled to write 50 lines of code instead of just 10.

Additionally, by utilizing procedures, errors related to word presence checks are localized within the procedure itself. In the absence of procedures, I would be required to scrutinize the entire program to pinpoint the source of any errors related to this functionality.

31.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called isEqual(value1, value2). The procedure returns true if the two parameters, value1 and value2, are equal in value and returns false otherwise. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses isEqual to count the number of times a certain value appears in your list. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else to write the program code.

1.Set the initial value of “total” to 0.

2.Set the initial value of count to 1.

3.Begin iterating through the “nums” list.

4.For each item in the list, check if “value” is equal to “nums\[count\],” where “count” varies from 1 to 10, and if a match is found, increase “total” by one.

5.Once all items in the list have been iterated through, return the cumulative “total” value.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f065-01.jpg)

32.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called math.min(value1, value2). The procedure returns true if value1 is less than value1 and returns false otherwise. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses math.min to find the minimum number in your list. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else to write the program code.

1.Begin by setting the initial value of minimum to the smaller of the two numbers at index 1 and index 2 in the nums list using the math.min procedure: minimum = math.min(nums\[1\], nums\[2\])

![Images](OEBPS/images/f065-00.jpg)

2.Begin iterating through the nums list.

3.For each item in the list, verify if the current item’s value is less than the value stored in minimum.

4.If it is, update the minimum value to the current item’s value.

5.Continue this process for all items in the list; then end the loop.

6.Once all the items in the list have been iterated through, the variable minimum will store the minimum value.

7.Return this value.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f066-01.jpg)

### Answer questions 33–47 based on the following procedure.

**Note:** Like the AP exam, the list used in the below code segment will start at index 1.

The function of the following code segment is to display “Winner” if the user enters a number that occurs more than 10 percent of the total dice rolls and “Loser” if it does not.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f067-01.jpg)

**Program Design, Function, and Purpose**

33.Identify an expected user of your program. Describe one way your program’s design meets the needs of this user.

My program is designed for students who have a need to be taught about the basics of statistics. Statistics is a required class in high school.

34.Explain how a program or code segment functions.

The function of my program is to prompt the user to pick a number from 1 to 6. The program will fill the list called diceRolls with 60 random numbers from 1 to 6. If the users number occurs more than 10 percent in the list, the program will return “Winner.” If not, it will return “Loser.”

35.Identify the input/output of the program.

The user provides an input, which is a number. If the input is found in the list more than 10 percent, the output is the word “Winner.” If not, the output is the word “Loser.”

**Algorithm Development**

36.Identify the number of times the body of your iteration statement will execute.

The loop in my program will run for 60 iterations.

37.Describe a condition or error that would cause your iteration statement to not terminate and cause an infinite loop. If no such condition or error exists, explain how the loop could be modified to cause an infinite loop.

The loop utilized in my program is a “for-each” loop, which inherently prevents infinite loops. A “for-each” loop automatically initiates at the first index and concludes at the final index, ensuring that it cannot result in an infinite loop. However, if I change the loop from “FOR EACH item IN diceRolls” to “REPEAT UNTIL pick = diceRolls\[count\]” where pick is not contained in the words list, an infinite loop would occur.

38.Describe two calls to the procedure in the supplied program. Each call must pass a different argument(s) that causes a different segment of code in the algorithm to execute, and describe what conditions are being tested and the result of each call.

Assume that diceRolls contains the numbers (1, 1, 3, 5, 6, 4), and pick contains the number 1. The first call would be . . . .

![Images](OEBPS/images/f068-01.jpg)

Assume that diceRolls contains the numbers (1, 1, 3, 5, 6, 4), and pick contains the number 5. The second call would be . . . .

![Images](OEBPS/images/f068-02.jpg)

The first call would test to see if “1” is contained in the diceRolls list more than 10 percent.

The second call would test to see if “5” is contained in the diceRolls list more than 10 percent.

The results of the first call are “Winner” will be displayed.

The results of the second call are “Loser” will be displayed.

39.Describe what the data contained in the list represents in your program.

My list holds 60 random numbers from 1 to 6, which represent 60 dice rolls.

**Errors and Testing**

40.Outline a modification that would result in a runtime error.

Entering a letter instead of a number would trigger a runtime error because the program wouldn’t be able to compare a letter for equality with a number.

41.Outline a modification that would result in a logical error.

Altering the condition from “\[IF \[total > 10\]\]” to “\[IF \[total > 50\]\]” would lead to the program returning “Winner” only if the pick occurred in the list more than 50 percent of the time, rather than the initial 10 percent. This change would introduce a logical error in the program’s behavior.

42.Outline a modification that would result in a syntax error.

Setting the initial value of total as “total = 1” rather than “total <− 1” in the code would result in a syntax error.

43.Outline a modification that would result in an overflow error.

If the data type of total were an 8-bit integer, the maximum value it could hold without triggering an overflow error would be 2^n − 1, which, in this specific case, is 256 − 1, equal to 255. Consequently, attempting to store the number 278 in total would result in an overflow error.

44.Describe the procedure used to test for correctness of your algorithm.

When testing with the scenario where diceRolls contained fifteen 1’s and pick is equal to 1, the expected outcome is “Winner,” and indeed, it was.

In another test case, when testing with the scenario where diceRolls contained seven 1’s and pick is equal to 1, the expected outcome is “Loser,” and indeed, it was.

**Data and Procedural Abstraction**

45.Elaborate on the role of the list within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a list, and discuss how employing the list has simplified the program’s complexity.

In my program, I employed a list named “diceRolls,” which comprises 60 numbers representing 60 random dice rolls from one to six. These numbers are stored in the list with indices ranging from 1 to 60. Utilizing this list allowed me to minimize the number of variables I needed to create, as I only had to name one variable, “diceRolls,” instead of having to define 60 individual variables for each planet. This reduction in code complexity, achieved by using a single variable to represent the list, significantly lowered the likelihood of errors in my program.

Moreover, through the utilization of my “diceRolls” list, I could efficiently examine and verify if the user’s input matched 10 percent of the numbers contained in the list by employing a for loop in conjunction with just one if statement. Without employing a list, testing for equality among all 60 rolls would necessitate the creation of 60 distinct if statements. This simplification of code complexity, facilitated by the use of a singular variable representing the list, considerably diminished the probability of errors in my program.

46.Elaborate on the role of procedural abstraction within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a procedural abstraction, and discuss how employing the procedural abstraction has simplified the program’s complexity.

By encapsulating the algorithm responsible for checking the presence of a word in a list within a procedure, I have significantly streamlined the complexity of my program. This simplification allows me to reuse the algorithm at multiple points in my program without the need for redundant code. For example, when I need to verify the presence of five words in my list, I can simply call the procedure five times. Without this procedural approach, I would be compelled to write 20 lines of code, as opposed to just five.

Furthermore, the utilization of procedures ensures that any errors associated with word presence checks are confined within the procedure itself. In the absence of procedures, identifying the source of errors related to this functionality would require a comprehensive examination of the entire program.

47.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called isEqual(value1, value2). The procedure returns true if the two parameters, value1 and value2, are equal in value and returns false otherwise. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses isEqual to count the number of times a certain value appears in your list. ### Answer questions 48–62 based on the following procedure.

**Note:** Like the AP exam, the list used in the below code segment will start at index 1.

The purpose of the provided procedure is to compute the charging costs, which are determined by the hour(s) during which the charging takes place. The “rates” table contains a list of 24 one-hour periods, numbered from 1 to 24, along with the associated hourly cost for each period. This procedure is designed to calculate the cumulative charging cost based on the specified starting time and the duration in hours.

![Images](OEBPS/images/f072-01.jpg)

**Program Design, Function, and Purpose**

48.Identify an expected user of your program. Describe one way your program’s design meets the needs of this user.

My program is tailored for electronic item users interested in determining the charging expenses for items such as cell phones or cars. It can compute the charging cost by taking into account the starting time and the duration of the charging session.

49.Explain how a program or code segment functions.

The function of my program is to request input from the user regarding the starting time and duration. Subsequently, the program will go through the “rates” list calculating the total charging cost, starting at the specified start time and extending for the given duration. It will cycle through the list as needed, looping back to index 1 when the index exceeds 24. After completing this loop for the specified number of durations, the procedure will return the cumulative total cost.

50.Identify the input/output of the program.

The program receives user input in the form of “startTime” and “duration,” both of which are numeric values. In response, it provides an output, which is the total charging cost represented as a numeric value.

**Algorithm Development**

51.Identify the number of times the body of your iteration statement will execute.

The loop within my program will execute for a number of iterations equal to the value provided by the user as “duration.”

52.Describe a condition or error that would cause your iteration statement to not terminate and cause an infinite loop. If no such condition or error exists, explain how the loop could be modified to cause an infinite loop.

The loop used in my program is configured to execute a specific number of times as determined by the “duration” variable, regardless of the program’s outcome. This approach guarantees that an infinite loop cannot occur.

However, if I were to modify the loop to a “REPEAT UNTIL \[startTime = startTime + duration\]”, it could lead to an infinite loop when “startTime + duration” exceeds 26. This is because “startTime” will never reach a value greater than 25 due to the conditional IF statement subtracting 24 when “startTime” equals 25.

53.Describe two calls to the procedure in the supplied program. Each call must pass a different argument(s) that causes a different segment of code in the algorithm to execute, and describe what conditions are being tested and the resulting result of each call.

Assume that rates contain \[12,12,13,12,9,9,7,7,6,8,9,6,6,5,5,5,4,6,7,12,11,11,12,11\], startTime contains 4, and duration contains 6. The first call would be . . . .

RateTable\[rates, startTime, duration\]

Assume that rates contain \[12,12,13,12,9,9,7,7,6,8,9,6,6,5,5,5,4,6,7,12,11,11,12,11\], startTime contains 20, and duration contains 10. The first call would be . . . .

RateTable\[rates, startTime, duration\]

The first call would calculate the total cost from index 4 to index 10.

The second call would calculate the total cost from index 20 and loop around to index 5.

The result of the first call is 50.

The result of the second call is 115.

54.Describe what the data contained in the list represents in your program.

My list holds 24 numbers representing charging cost from hour 1 to hour 24.

**Errors and Testing**

55.Outline a modification that would result in a runtime error.

Attempting to access an array element with an index that falls outside the acceptable range of indices can lead to an error. For instance, within the mystery procedure, the words list contains 7 elements indexed from 1 to 7. Trying to access an element at index 8 or index 0 would result in a runtime error. If the “REPEAT 7 times” command were changed to “REPEAT 8 times,” it would result in “words\[count\]” to contain words\[8\], which exceeds the valid bounds of the words list.

56.Outline a modification that would result in a logical error.

Altering the conditional statement from “IF startTime = 25” to “IF startTime > 20” would introduce a logic error. This change would render the hours greater than 20 inaccessible, affecting the program’s results.

57.Outline a modification that would result in a syntax error.

Setting the initial value of cost as “cost = 0” rather than “cost <− 1” in the code would result in a syntax error.

58.Outline a modification that would result in an overflow error.

If the data type of cost were an 8-bit integer, the maximum value it could hold without triggering an overflow error would be 2^n − 1, which, in this specific case, is 256 − 1, equal to 255. Consequently, attempting to store the number 278 in cost would result in an overflow error.

59.Describe the procedure used to test for correctness of your algorithm.

Testing both conditions of startTime not reaching 25 and startTime reaching 25.

Assume that rates contain \[12,12,13,12,9,9,7,7,6,8,9,6,6,5,5,5,4,6,7,12,11,11,12,11\], startTime contains 4, and duration contains 6. The first call should be 20, and it was.

Assume that rates contain \[12,12,13,12,9,9,7,7,6,8,9,6,6,5,5,5,4,6,7,12,11,11,12,11\], startTime contains 20, and duration contains 10. The first call should be 115, and it was.

**Data and Procedural Abstraction**

60.Elaborate on the role of the list within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a list, and discuss how employing the list has simplified the program’s complexity.

In my program, I employed a list named “rates,” which comprises 24 numbers signifying the rate charges for a 24-hour period. These rates are stored in the list with indices ranging from 1 to 24. Utilizing this list allowed me to minimize the number of variables I needed to create, as I only had to name one variable, “rates”, instead of having to define 24 individual variables for each hour. This reduction in code complexity, achieved by using a single variable to represent the list, significantly lowered the likelihood of errors in my program.

Moreover, through the utilization of my “rates” list, I could efficiently examine and calculate any element within the list by employing a for loop in conjunction with just one if statement. Without employing a list, I would need 24 distinct if statements. This simplification of code complexity, facilitated by the use of a singular variable representing the list, considerably diminished the probability of errors in my program.

61.Elaborate on the role of procedural abstraction within your program. Explain how the program’s structure would differ if it did not use a procedural abstraction, and discuss how employing the procedural abstraction has simplified the program’s complexity.

By encapsulating the algorithm responsible for calculating the total charging cost, I have effectively streamlined the complexity of my program. This simplification allows me to reuse the algorithm at various junctures within my program without having to duplicate the code. For example, when the need arises to calculate multiple costs, I can simply invoke the procedure multiple times.

Furthermore, the use of procedures ensures that any errors associated with word presence checks are confined within the procedure itself. Without procedures, identifying the source of errors linked to this functionality would necessitate a comprehensive examination of the entire program.

62.Suppose you are provided with a procedure called isEqual(value1, value2). The procedure returns true if the two parameters, value1 and value2, are equal in value and returns false otherwise. Using the list you identified in the List section of your Personalized Project Reference, explain in detailed steps an algorithm that uses isEqual to count the number of times a certain value appears in your list. Your explanation must be detailed enough for someone else to write the program code.

1.Set the initial value of “total” to 0.

2.Begin iterating through the “rates” list.

3.For each item in the list, check if “rates\[startTime\]” is equal to the supplied value, and if a match is found, increase “total” by one.

4.Once all items in the list have been iterated through, return the cumulative “total” value.
